It’s these small clinical imperfections — a note I could have worded more clearly, a question I maybe should have asked sooner, something I double-check later and think, “Why didn’t I do that perfectly the first time?” And then I replay it over and over, like it’s evidence. Like it proves I’m dangerous, or that I’m somehow fooling everyone by still being allowed to take care of people.\n\nI know, saying it out loud, it sounds… maybe extreme. But it feels very real when I’m in it. I keep coming back to this feeling that I’ve failed in some fundamental way, even when other people don’t seem to see it that way. That’s what brought me here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these thoughts about your performance come into your mind in a way that feels intrusive or unwanted, almost like they pop up without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, they do. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Let me review all the ways I might be inadequate today.” It just sort of arrives, and once it’s there, it’s hard to get out from under it.\n\nI’ll be doing something ordinary — charting, walking between rooms, even just thinking back over the day — and suddenly I’ll remember some small thing, like maybe I phrased a note imperfectly or I didn’t ask a question in exactly the order I think I should have, and it becomes this whole cascade. I start replaying it again and again, and it doesn’t stay small. It starts feeling like evidence that I shouldn’t be trusted, like I’m a dangerous doctor and everyone is just not seeing it yet.\n\nI don’t want the thoughts. I really don’t. They feel intrusive in that sense. But when they come up, they also feel… convincing. Like I’m not just worrying, I’m recognizing something true about myself. That I don’t deserve the trust patients and colleagues put in me.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do you find yourself doing anything — either physically or mentally — to try to reduce the discomfort they bring, like checking things over and over, seeking reassurance, or repeating certain thoughts in your head?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean… I think I do try to make the feeling go away, but I don’t know if it’s in the way you mean.\n\nI’ll sometimes go back over things in my head, but that also just feels like worrying, not like a deliberate ritual. Like I’ll think, “Okay, did I actually miss something, or am I exaggerating?” and then I kind of mentally retrace what happened. But I don’t always come out feeling better. Sometimes it just makes the whole thing feel more real.\n\nAs for checking over and over or reassurance… I don’t know. I’m hesitant to say yes because I’m not sure I have some specific thing I do every time. It’s more that I get stuck with the discomfort and keep trying to reason my way out of it, and then I don’t really get anywhere.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts upset you, do you find yourself deliberately doing something — like mentally reviewing events over and over, checking things with others, or trying to 'neutralize' the thought with another thought — in order to feel less anxious or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that I do it deliberately in that structured way. That’s the part I’m having trouble answering, because when you say “neutralize” or “prevent something bad,” it sounds like a clear action, like I have a routine I recognize as a routine. I don’t think I do.\n\nWhat happens is more… I get pulled into reviewing. I’ll go back through the encounter or the note or the decision in my mind, and I’ll try to figure out whether the feeling is justified. But I don’t experience it as, “If I think this thought three times, nothing bad will happen,” or anything like that. It feels more like I’m trying to get certainty, I suppose, or trying to convince myself that I’m not as unsafe as I feel. But then the reviewing doesn’t really settle it. Sometimes it makes me feel worse, because I find another angle to criticize.\n\nI don’t think I’m checking with other people in a repeated, ritualized way, at least not that I’ve noticed. And I’m not sure I’m doing something to cancel the thought out. It’s more like I can’t leave it alone once it starts, but I don’t know that it has the quality of a deliberate thing I choose to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to go over things in your mind repeatedly, like rechecking decisions or replaying events, even if it’s not something you want to do, just to try to feel more certain or reduce the anxiety those thoughts bring up?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s closer to what happens. I do feel driven to go back over things, even when I don’t want to. It’s like I can’t just let a small clinical imperfection stay small.\n\nIf I think I may have worded something poorly, or made a decision that wasn’t perfect, or missed some nuance in an interaction, I’ll replay it in my mind over and over. Not necessarily because I want to, and not because it actually helps very much, but because I’m trying to get to some point where I can say, “Okay, that was acceptable. You’re not dangerous. You didn’t betray the trust people put in you.” But I usually don’t get that certainty.\n\nAnd the hard part is that it starts to feel like more than just anxiety. It starts to feel like proof. Like these little things are evidence that I shouldn’t be trusted as a doctor, that I don’t deserve the confidence patients or colleagues have in me. So I keep turning it over, looking for reassurance in the facts, I guess, but the more I examine it, the more it feels like I’m finding reasons to condemn myself.\n\nDoctor: When you replay these moments in your mind, do you feel that you have to do it a certain way or a certain number of times in order to feel relief or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of a number, or a specific sequence. I don’t have a rule like, “I need to replay it five times,” or “I have to start from the beginning and go through it exactly correctly or something bad will happen.”\n\nIt’s more loose than that, which almost makes it harder to describe. I’ll circle around the same moment from different angles — what I said, what I should have said, how it might have been interpreted, whether I missed something obvious — but I’m not doing it in a prescribed way. I’m just trying to reach some kind of feeling of certainty or relief, and it doesn’t really arrive cleanly.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I feel like I have to do it a certain way to prevent something bad. It’s more like I can’t quite stop until I’m exhausted or distracted, or until the urgency fades a little. But there isn’t a ritual to it that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a strong need to mentally go over things in a specific way or in response to a specific type of thought, even if it’s not a strict ritual, just to try to feel better or reduce the discomfort those thoughts bring?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can identify a specific type of thought where I then have to respond in a particular mental way. It’s not like, “If I have this thought, I must review it like this.” At least, I haven’t noticed that kind of pattern.\n\nWhat I do know is that certain things seem to catch in my mind more easily — usually things where I feel I might have failed in some way, or where there’s uncertainty and I can’t quite prove to myself that everything was okay. Then I start turning it over. But it doesn’t feel organized, and it doesn’t feel like a technique I use to reduce discomfort exactly. It’s more like I get stuck trying to understand whether the discomfort is justified.\n\nSo… I guess the answer is probably no, not in the way you mean. I don’t have a specific mental procedure. I do go over things, but I haven’t noticed a fixed trigger-and-response pattern beyond just feeling unsettled and unable to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to mentally reanalyze or re-examine past decisions in detail — even if not in a fixed way — as a way to manage the anxiety that comes up when these intrusive thoughts occur?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say that exactly. I mean… I do re-examine decisions, sometimes in a lot of detail, but I’m not sure I experience it as a deliberate way to manage anxiety, if that distinction makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like the anxiety or discomfort is already there, and then my mind starts trying to solve the situation — almost like if I can reconstruct it carefully enough, I’ll find the piece that tells me whether I was right or wrong. But I don’t sit down and think, “I’m going to reanalyze this so I can feel better.” It’s less intentional than that.\n\nAnd it isn’t always past decisions in some formal sense. Sometimes it’s a phrase I used, or whether I should have noticed something, or whether I handled an interaction appropriately. Then I’ll go back through it, but it feels kind of messy and circular, not like a clear review process.\n\nSo… yes, I do get pulled into re-examining things, but I’m hesitant to say I have to do it as a way to manage intrusive thoughts. I’m not sure I’ve noticed it that cleanly. It’s more that I get unsettled and then I can’t easily leave the question alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself mentally reanalyzing or reviewing past decisions or actions repeatedly because you feel that not doing so might lead to increased anxiety, guilt, or a sense of being unsafe as a physician?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s closer to it. Not in the sense that I think, “If I don’t review this three times, something will happen,” but more that if I don’t go back over it, I’m left with this feeling that I’m being irresponsible by not checking myself.\n\nEspecially with clinical things — even small ones. Like a wording choice in a note, or whether I framed something clearly enough to a patient, or whether I should have asked one more question even if, objectively, it was probably fine. I’ll replay it as if that little imperfection is evidence of something much bigger. Like it isn’t just “I could have done that slightly better,” it becomes, “Maybe this proves I don’t deserve the trust people are putting in me.”\n\nAnd that’s really the thing I’m here about, I think. I keep feeling like I’m not worthy of being trusted in that role. Like people see me as safe or competent because I have the title, but I know every small hesitation or imperfect choice underneath it. So when I review things, it’s partly anxiety, but it’s also guilt. It feels like if I stop looking at it, I’m letting myself off too easily.\n\nI don’t know if I’d say I feel “unsafe” exactly as a physician, but… I do sometimes feel like I could be dangerous because I’m not perfect. And then the reviewing becomes this attempt to prove that I’m not — or sometimes it just ends up proving the opposite in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or mental acts — like reviewing conversations, replaying events, or analyzing decisions — in an attempt to prevent feeling anxious or guilty, or to make sure you don’t make a mistake that could harm someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though it doesn’t feel as clean or intentional as “I’m doing this to prevent anxiety.” It feels more like I can’t let go of something because if I don’t examine it closely, then I’m somehow being careless.\n\nA lot of it is around work. I’ll replay a conversation with a patient, or a decision I made, or even something small in documentation, and it starts to feel much larger than it probably was. Like a small imperfection becomes evidence that I shouldn’t be trusted. I’ll think, “Why did I phrase it that way?” or “Should I have asked one more question?” or “What if I missed something because I was tired or distracted?” And then I’ll go through it again and again, trying to decide whether it was actually okay.\n\nBut it’s not reassuring for very long, if at all. Sometimes it just makes me feel worse, because the more I look at it, the more I find ways it could have been better. And then it turns into this feeling that maybe I’m dangerous in that role, not because there’s one clear mistake I can point to, but because I’m not perfect and people are trusting me anyway.\n\nSo… yes, I replay and analyze things. I think part of me is trying to make sure I didn’t harm someone or that I’m not going to. But underneath that, it’s more this guilt, this feeling that I don’t deserve the trust people put in me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that you have to repeat these mental reviews or analyses in order to prevent something bad from happening, like making a mistake that could harm someone or feeling unbearable guilt?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it that strongly. I don’t have a clear sense of, “I must do this review or something bad will happen,” like a rule in my head. It’s more vague than that.\n\nThere are times where if I don’t go back over something, I feel very unsettled, like I’m being negligent by moving on too quickly. And the guilt can feel… disproportionate, I guess. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed a direct link where I’m repeating the review specifically to prevent a bad outcome. It’s more that the question won’t leave me alone, and if I try to ignore it, it feels like I’m avoiding responsibility.\n\nSo yes, the reviews can feel hard to stop, and they do seem tied to fear of mistakes or guilt. But “in order to prevent something bad from happening” — I’m not completely sure. It doesn’t feel that organized in my mind. It feels more like I get stuck trying to find certainty, and I don’t really get it.\n\nDoctor: When you have these intrusive thoughts, do you feel a strong internal pressure to perform certain mental acts—like reviewing events, analyzing decisions, or replaying conversations—because you believe that not doing so could lead to increased anxiety, guilt, or a mistake that might harm someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… there is pressure, but I think it’s less like a clear command and more like this heavy internal demand that I can’t just move on. It’s not, “If I don’t replay this exact conversation, then X will happen,” but it’s more like, “If I don’t examine this, then I’m the kind of person who shouldn’t be trusted with patients.”\n\nThat’s really what I keep coming back to. I’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. And when I think about work, even very small clinical imperfections start to feel enormous. Something like whether I worded something the right way, or whether I paused too long, or whether I should have asked one more clarifying question — it doesn’t stay as just a small thing. I replay it as if it proves something about me, like it proves I’m dangerous as a doctor.\n\nSo yes, when the thought comes up, I do feel pushed to review it and analyze it. I think part of me is trying to reduce the guilt, or make sure I didn’t miss something that could hurt someone. But honestly, a lot of the time it feels like I’m trying to answer this bigger question of whether I should be in this role at all. And the reviewing doesn’t really settle it. It just keeps circling around the same fear: that people trust me because of my title, but if they saw all the small doubts and imperfections underneath, they wouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you absolutely must finish reviewing a thought or situation in your mind, even if you don’t want to, because not doing so would feel intolerable or would mean you’re failing in your responsibility as a doctor?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s close, though the word “finish” is difficult, because I don’t know that it ever feels finished. It’s more like I keep trying to reach a point where I can say, “Okay, that wasn’t dangerous, that doesn’t mean I failed,” but I don’t really get there.\n\nWhen something small happens clinically — or even when I just remember something later and wonder if it was imperfect — I can feel this need to go back over it in my mind. Not always because there’s a concrete error, but because it starts to feel like the imperfection itself means something about me. Like, if I phrased something badly, or didn’t think of every possible angle, then maybe that proves I shouldn’t be trusted.\n\nAnd that’s the part that feels intolerable, I guess. Not just anxiety, but the guilt of moving on as if it didn’t matter. I’ll think, “If I stop reviewing this, am I just excusing myself? Am I being careless because I don’t want to face what I did?” So I keep turning it over, even when I’m tired of it, even when I know I’m probably not getting anywhere.\n\nI’m here because… I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. And when I replay these small clinical imperfections, they don’t stay small. They start to feel like evidence that I’m a dangerous doctor, or that I’m pretending to be safe and competent when maybe I’m not. So yes, sometimes stopping the review feels like failing in my responsibility, even if I can also see that the reviewing itself isn’t really helping.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that you have to keep analyzing or replaying certain thoughts or events in your mind, even when you don’t want to, because not doing so would increase your anxiety or make you feel like you're at fault or responsible for something bad happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, it’s not always as neat as “if I don’t replay this, then something bad will happen,” but emotionally it feels close to that. If I don’t go back over it, I feel like I’m letting myself off too easily, or like I’m ignoring something I should be taking responsibility for.\n\nA lot of it is tied to work. I’m here because I feel like I don’t really deserve the trust people put in me. And then these small things—small clinical imperfections, things that maybe another person would just learn from and move on from—become huge in my head. I’ll replay whether I said something the right way, whether I missed a nuance, whether I should have asked one more question, whether my judgment was as careful as it should have been. And it starts to feel like those little things are not just little things, but evidence that I’m dangerous as a doctor.\n\nSo when I keep analyzing it, part of me is trying to figure out if I’m at fault, or if I’ve failed someone, or if I’m the kind of person who shouldn’t be trusted in that role. And if I stop before I’ve somehow resolved it—which I usually don’t—it feels irresponsible. Like I’m choosing comfort over accountability.\n\nI don’t want to keep doing it. It’s exhausting and it doesn’t really reassure me for long. But stopping can feel worse, because then I’m left with this sense that maybe I’m just avoiding the truth about myself.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up about not being good enough or being dangerous as a doctor, do they feel out of line with how you see yourself, or do they feel like something you actually believe is true about you?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to answer that, because part of me knows how extreme it sounds when I say it out loud. I can look at the chart afterward, and there may be nothing there — no clear bad outcome, no note that something went wrong, no evidence that anyone was harmed. And if someone else described the same situation to me, I might be able to say, “That was routine,” or “That delay was not dangerous,” or “That message wasn’t perfect, but it wasn’t harmful.”\n\nBut when it’s me, it doesn’t feel only like an anxious thought that’s separate from who I am. It starts to feel true. Like there’s this quiet conviction that I’ve harmed someone in some subtle way and it just hasn’t been discovered yet.\n\nI’ll think about some routine delay, or a message I sent that wasn’t worded perfectly, or something I didn’t respond to as quickly or as completely as I think I should have. And even if I review it and there’s no sign of harm, I still keep thinking, “No, something happened because of this. Someone was affected. It just hasn’t surfaced yet.” And then I imagine that eventually everyone is going to find out — the patient, the team, whoever — and they’ll see that I was careless or dangerous all along.\n\nSo I guess… it feels both out of line and not out of line. Intellectually, I can see that the evidence isn’t there. But emotionally, and sometimes more than emotionally, it feels like something I actually believe about myself: that I’m not just imperfect, but that I’ve quietly hurt someone and it’s only a matter of time before it’s discovered.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It feels like it fits, unfortunately. I don’t like saying that, because I know there’s a version of me that can step back and say, “You’re taking small things and making them mean something enormous.” But when I’m actually in it, it doesn’t feel foreign, like some random thought that’s obviously separate from me. It feels like it’s revealing something true.

I’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. That’s really the center of it. People come to me assuming I’m careful, competent, safe — and when I replay these small clinical imperfections, they start to feel like proof that I’m not. Like if I missed a nuance, or worded something poorly, or didn’t think through every possible implication, then maybe that isn’t just being human. Maybe it means I’m dangerous, or unworthy of the role.

And I know that may sound disproportionate. I can see that. But it doesn’t land as “just anxiety” in the moment. It feels consistent with this deeper fear I have about myself professionally — that I’m not deserving of the confidence patients and colleagues place in me. So no, it doesn’t feel foreign exactly. It feels painfully believable.

But stopping can feel worse, because then I’m left with this sense that maybe I’m just avoiding the truth about myself.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up about not being good enough or being dangerous as a doctor, do they feel out of line with how you see yourself, or do they feel like something you actually believe is true about you?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to answer that, because part of me knows how extreme it sounds when I say it out loud. I can look at the chart afterward, and there may be nothing there — no clear bad outcome, no note that something went wrong, no evidence that anyone was harmed. And if someone else described the same situation to me, I might be able to say, “That was routine,” or “That delay was not dangerous,” or “That message wasn’t perfect, but it wasn’t harmful.”\n\nBut when it’s me, it doesn’t feel only like an anxious thought that’s separate from who I am. It starts to feel true. Like there’s this quiet conviction that I’ve harmed someone in some subtle way and it just hasn’t been discovered yet.\n\nI’ll think about some routine delay, or a message I sent that wasn’t worded perfectly, or something I didn’t respond to as quickly or as completely as I think I should have. And even if I review it and there’s no sign of harm, I still keep thinking, “No, something happened because of this. Someone was affected. It just hasn’t surfaced yet.” And then I imagine that eventually everyone is going to find out — the patient, the team, whoever — and they’ll see that I was careless or dangerous all along.\n\nSo I guess… it feels both out of line and not out of line. Intellectually, I can see that the evidence isn’t there. But emotionally, and sometimes more than emotionally, it feels like something I actually believe about myself: that I’m not just imperfect, but that I’ve quietly hurt someone and it’s only a matter of time before it’s discovered.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts about being a dangerous or unworthy doctor come up, do they feel like something foreign to you that you don’t truly believe, or do they feel like they fit with how you see yourself as a person or professional?\n\nPatient: It feels like it fits, unfortunately. I don’t like saying that, because I know there’s a version of me that can step back and say, “You’re taking small things and making them mean something enormous.” But when I’m actually in it, it doesn’t feel foreign, like some random thought that’s obviously separate from me. It feels like it’s revealing something true.\n\nI’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. That’s really the center of it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I do. It’s not always a formal checklist or anything — I don’t sit there and say, “Now I will review step one, step two, step three.” But I end up doing that in practice. I’ll go back through a decision or an interaction in this very detailed way, almost like I’m auditing myself, trying to find the point where I failed.

And it’s usually these small things. A phrase I used with a patient, or whether I responded quickly enough, or whether I should have considered something one step further. On paper, it might look minor. But in my head it becomes this whole chain of implications: if I missed that nuance, then maybe I wasn’t careful; if I wasn’t careful, maybe I harmed someone; if I harmed someone, then maybe I shouldn’t be trusted as a doctor.

So yes, the reanalyzing is partly about anxiety. I think I’m trying to reduce it by proving to myself that I didn’t do something wrong. But it doesn’t really work, because the more I replay it, the more it starts to feel like evidence against me. Like these small imperfections are actually proof that I’m dangerous or that I don’t deserve the trust people put in me.

And if I try to stop before I’ve gone through it enough, I feel this pressure, like I’m being irresponsible or letting myself avoid accountability. So I keep turning it over and over, even though I know it’s exhausting and usually doesn’t lead anywhere reassuring.

Not always because there’s a concrete error, but because it starts to feel like the imperfection itself means something about me. Like, if I phrased something badly, or didn’t think of every possible angle, then maybe that proves I shouldn’t be trusted.\n\nAnd that’s the part that feels intolerable, I guess. Not just anxiety, but the guilt of moving on as if it didn’t matter. I’ll think, “If I stop reviewing this, am I just excusing myself? Am I being careless because I don’t want to face what I did?” So I keep turning it over, even when I’m tired of it, even when I know I’m probably not getting anywhere.\n\nI’m here because… I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. And when I replay these small clinical imperfections, they don’t stay small. They start to feel like evidence that I’m a dangerous doctor, or that I’m pretending to be safe and competent when maybe I’m not. So yes, sometimes stopping the review feels like failing in my responsibility, even if I can also see that the reviewing itself isn’t really helping.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that you have to keep analyzing or replaying certain thoughts or events in your mind, even when you don’t want to, because not doing so would increase your anxiety or make you feel like you're at fault or responsible for something bad happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, it’s not always as neat as “if I don’t replay this, then something bad will happen,” but emotionally it feels close to that. If I don’t go back over it, I feel like I’m letting myself off too easily, or like I’m ignoring something I should be taking responsibility for.\n\nA lot of it is tied to work. I’m here because I feel like I don’t really deserve the trust people put in me. And then these small things—small clinical imperfections, things that maybe another person would just learn from and move on from—become huge in my head. I’ll replay whether I said something the right way, whether I missed a nuance, whether I should have asked one more question, whether my judgment was as careful as it should have been. And it starts to feel like those little things are not just little things, but evidence that I’m dangerous as a doctor.\n\nSo when I keep analyzing it, part of me is trying to figure out if I’m at fault, or if I’ve failed someone, or if I’m the kind of person who shouldn’t be trusted in that role. And if I stop before I’ve somehow resolved it—which I usually don’t—it feels irresponsible. Like I’m choosing comfort over accountability.\n\nI don’t want to keep doing it. It’s exhausting and it doesn’t really reassure me for long. But stopping can feel worse, because then I’m left with this sense that maybe I’m just avoiding the truth about myself.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up about not being good enough or being dangerous as a doctor, do they feel out of line with how you see yourself, or do they feel like something you actually believe is true about you?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to answer that, because part of me knows how extreme it sounds when I say it out loud. I can look at the chart afterward, and there may be nothing there — no clear bad outcome, no note that something went wrong, no evidence that anyone was harmed. And if someone else described the same situation to me, I might be able to say, “That was routine,” or “That delay was not dangerous,” or “That message wasn’t perfect, but it wasn’t harmful.”\n\nBut when it’s me, it doesn’t feel only like an anxious thought that’s separate from who I am. It starts to feel true. Like there’s this quiet conviction that I’ve harmed someone in some subtle way and it just hasn’t been discovered yet.\n\nI’ll think about some routine delay, or a message I sent that wasn’t worded perfectly, or something I didn’t respond to as quickly or as completely as I think I should have. And even if I review it and there’s no sign of harm, I still keep thinking, “No, something happened because of this. Someone was affected. It just hasn’t surfaced yet.” And then I imagine that eventually everyone is going to find out — the patient, the team, whoever — and they’ll see that I was careless or dangerous all along.\n\nSo I guess… it feels both out of line and not out of line. Intellectually, I can see that the evidence isn’t there. But emotionally, and sometimes more than emotionally, it feels like something I actually believe about myself: that I’m not just imperfect, but that I’ve quietly hurt someone and it’s only a matter of time before it’s discovered.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts about being a dangerous or unworthy doctor come up, do they feel like something foreign to you that you don’t truly believe, or do they feel like they fit with how you see yourself as a person or professional?\n\nPatient: It feels like it fits, unfortunately. I don’t like saying that, because I know there’s a version of me that can step back and say, “You’re taking small things and making them mean something enormous.” But when I’m actually in it, it doesn’t feel foreign, like some random thought that’s obviously separate from me. It feels like it’s revealing something true.\n\nI’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. That’s really the center of it. People come to me assuming I’m careful, competent, safe — and when I replay these small clinical imperfections, they start to feel like proof that I’m not. Like if I missed a nuance, or worded something poorly, or didn’t think through every possible implication, then maybe that isn’t just being human. Maybe it means I’m dangerous, or unworthy of the role.\n\nAnd I know that may sound disproportionate. I can see that. But it doesn’t land as “just anxiety” in the moment. It feels consistent with this deeper fear I have about myself professionally — that I’m not deserving of the confidence patients and colleagues place in me. So no, it doesn’t feel foreign exactly. It feels painfully believable.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you have to mentally reanalyze or re-examine past decisions in detail — even if not in a fixed way — as a way to manage the anxiety that comes up when these intrusive thoughts occur?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. It’s not always a formal checklist or anything — I don’t sit there and say, “Now I will review step one, step two, step three.” But I end up doing that in practice. I’ll go back through a decision or an interaction in this very detailed way, almost like I’m auditing myself, trying to find the point where I failed.\n\nAnd it’s usually these small things. A phrase I used with a patient, or whether I responded quickly enough, or whether I should have considered something one step further. On paper, it might look minor. But in my head it becomes this whole chain of implications: if I missed that nuance, then maybe I wasn’t careful; if I wasn’t careful, maybe I harmed someone; if I harmed someone, then maybe I shouldn’t be trusted as a doctor.\n\nSo yes, the reanalyzing is partly about anxiety. I think I’m trying to reduce it by proving to myself that I didn’t do something wrong. But it doesn’t really work, because the more I replay it, the more it starts to feel like evidence against me. Like these small imperfections are actually proof that I’m dangerous or that I don’t deserve the trust people put in me.\n\nAnd if I try to stop before I’ve gone through it enough, I feel this pressure, like I’m being irresponsible or letting myself avoid accountability. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think that is a big part of it. It’s not just that I review things because I’m curious or because I want to learn from them in a normal professional way. It feels driven, like I’m supposed to go back through every small imperfection until I can be sure I didn’t miss something harmful.

And the reason I’m here is really that I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. So when some small clinical thing happens — a message I sent, a delay, a phrase I used, a decision that maybe could have been more complete — I don’t experience it as just a minor imperfection. I start replaying it like it might be proof that I’m a dangerous doctor.

If I don’t review it, I feel like I’m being careless all over again. Like not rechecking it means I’m choosing to ignore the possibility that I harmed someone, or that I could harm someone. And then the guilt gets worse, because it feels like I’m letting myself off the hook when I don’t deserve to be let off the hook.

So yes, I do feel pushed to analyze and replay things. I’ll go over the sequence in my head — what I said, what I didn’t say, what I should have noticed, what someone might have taken from it — and I’m trying to get to a place where I can say, “No, this was safe, this was okay.” But often it doesn’t reassure me. It just makes the whole thing feel more serious, like the fact that I have to examine it so much means there must be something there.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main reason I came in is that I’ve been feeling like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. I’m a physician, and people come to me assuming I’m competent and safe and worthy of that responsibility, and lately I just keep thinking, almost obsessively, that I’m not.\n\nIt’s not even always big things. It’s these small clinical imperfections — a note I could have worded more clearly, a question I maybe should have asked sooner, something I double-check later and think, “Why didn’t I do that perfectly the first time?” And then I replay it over and over, like it’s evidence. Like it proves I’m dangerous, or that I’m somehow fooling everyone by still being allowed to take care of people.\n\nI know, saying it out loud, it sounds… maybe extreme. But it feels very real when I’m in it. I keep coming back to this feeling that I’ve failed in some fundamental way, even when other people don’t seem to see it that way. That’s what brought me here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these thoughts about your performance come into your mind in a way that feels intrusive or unwanted, almost like they pop up without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, they do. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Let me review all the ways I might be inadequate today.” It just sort of arrives, and once it’s there, it’s hard to get out from under it.\n\nI’ll be doing something ordinary — charting, walking between rooms, even just thinking back over the day — and suddenly I’ll remember some small thing, like maybe I phrased a note imperfectly or I didn’t ask a question in exactly the order I think I should have, and it becomes this whole cascade. I start replaying it again and again, and it doesn’t stay small. It starts feeling like evidence that I shouldn’t be trusted, like I’m a dangerous doctor and everyone is just not seeing it yet.\n\nI don’t want the thoughts. I really don’t. They feel intrusive in that sense. But when they come up, they also feel… convincing. Like I’m not just worrying, I’m recognizing something true about myself. That I don’t deserve the trust patients and colleagues put in me.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do you find yourself doing anything — either physically or mentally — to try to reduce the discomfort they bring, like checking things over and over, seeking reassurance, or repeating certain thoughts in your head?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean… I think I do try to make the feeling go away, but I don’t know if it’s in the way you mean.\n\nI’ll sometimes go back over things in my head, but that also just feels like worrying, not like a deliberate ritual. Like I’ll think, “Okay, did I actually miss something, or am I exaggerating?” and then I kind of mentally retrace what happened. But I don’t always come out feeling better. Sometimes it just makes the whole thing feel more real.\n\nAs for checking over and over or reassurance… I don’t know. I’m hesitant to say yes because I’m not sure I have some specific thing I do every time. It’s more that I get stuck with the discomfort and keep trying to reason my way out of it, and then I don’t really get anywhere.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts upset you, do you find yourself deliberately doing something — like mentally reviewing events over and over, checking things with others, or trying to 'neutralize' the thought with another thought — in order to feel less anxious or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that I do it deliberately in that structured way. That’s the part I’m having trouble answering, because when you say “neutralize” or “prevent something bad,” it sounds like a clear action, like I have a routine I recognize as a routine. I don’t think I do.\n\nWhat happens is more… I get pulled into reviewing. I’ll go back through the encounter or the note or the decision in my mind, and I’ll try to figure out whether the feeling is justified. But I don’t experience it as, “If I think this thought three times, nothing bad will happen,” or anything like that. It feels more like I’m trying to get certainty, I suppose, or trying to convince myself that I’m not as unsafe as I feel. But then the reviewing doesn’t really settle it. Sometimes it makes me feel worse, because I find another angle to criticize.\n\nI don’t think I’m checking with other people in a repeated, ritualized way, at least not that I’ve noticed. And I’m not sure I’m doing something to cancel the thought out. It’s more like I can’t leave it alone once it starts, but I don’t know that it has the quality of a deliberate thing I choose to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to go over things in your mind repeatedly, like rechecking decisions or replaying events, even if it’s not something you want to do, just to try to feel more certain or reduce the anxiety those thoughts bring up?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s closer to what happens. I do feel driven to go back over things, even when I don’t want to. It’s like I can’t just let a small clinical imperfection stay small.\n\nIf I think I may have worded something poorly, or made a decision that wasn’t perfect, or missed some nuance in an interaction, I’ll replay it in my mind over and over. Not necessarily because I want to, and not because it actually helps very much, but because I’m trying to get to some point where I can say, “Okay, that was acceptable. You’re not dangerous. You didn’t betray the trust people put in you.” But I usually don’t get that certainty.\n\nAnd the hard part is that it starts to feel like more than just anxiety. It starts to feel like proof. Like these little things are evidence that I shouldn’t be trusted as a doctor, that I don’t deserve the confidence patients or colleagues have in me. So I keep turning it over, looking for reassurance in the facts, I guess, but the more I examine it, the more it feels like I’m finding reasons to condemn myself.\n\nDoctor: When you replay these moments in your mind, do you feel that you have to do it a certain way or a certain number of times in order to feel relief or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of a number, or a specific sequence. I don’t have a rule like, “I need to replay it five times,” or “I have to start from the beginning and go through it exactly correctly or something bad will happen.”\n\nIt’s more loose than that, which almost makes it harder to describe. I’ll circle around the same moment from different angles — what I said, what I should have said, how it might have been interpreted, whether I missed something obvious — but I’m not doing it in a prescribed way. I’m just trying to reach some kind of feeling of certainty or relief, and it doesn’t really arrive cleanly.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I feel like I have to do it a certain way to prevent something bad. It’s more like I can’t quite stop until I’m exhausted or distracted, or until the urgency fades a little. But there isn’t a ritual to it that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a strong need to mentally go over things in a specific way or in response to a specific type of thought, even if it’s not a strict ritual, just to try to feel better or reduce the discomfort those thoughts bring?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can identify a specific type of thought where I then have to respond in a particular mental way. It’s not like, “If I have this thought, I must review it like this.” At least, I haven’t noticed that kind of pattern.\n\nWhat I do know is that certain things seem to catch in my mind more easily — usually things where I feel I might have failed in some way, or where there’s uncertainty and I can’t quite prove to myself that everything was okay. Then I start turning it over. But it doesn’t feel organized, and it doesn’t feel like a technique I use to reduce discomfort exactly. It’s more like I get stuck trying to understand whether the discomfort is justified.\n\nSo… I guess the answer is probably no, not in the way you mean. I don’t have a specific mental procedure. I do go over things, but I haven’t noticed a fixed trigger-and-response pattern beyond just feeling unsettled and unable to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to mentally reanalyze or re-examine past decisions in detail — even if not in a fixed way — as a way to manage the anxiety that comes up when these intrusive thoughts occur?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say that exactly. I mean… I do re-examine decisions, sometimes in a lot of detail, but I’m not sure I experience it as a deliberate way to manage anxiety, if that distinction makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like the anxiety or discomfort is already there, and then my mind starts trying to solve the situation — almost like if I can reconstruct it carefully enough, I’ll find the piece that tells me whether I was right or wrong. But I don’t sit down and think, “I’m going to reanalyze this so I can feel better.” It’s less intentional than that.\n\nAnd it isn’t always past decisions in some formal sense. Sometimes it’s a phrase I used, or whether I should have noticed something, or whether I handled an interaction appropriately. Then I’ll go back through it, but it feels kind of messy and circular, not like a clear review process.\n\nSo… yes, I do get pulled into re-examining things, but I’m hesitant to say I have to do it as a way to manage intrusive thoughts. I’m not sure I’ve noticed it that cleanly. It’s more that I get unsettled and then I can’t easily leave the question alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself mentally reanalyzing or reviewing past decisions or actions repeatedly because you feel that not doing so might lead to increased anxiety, guilt, or a sense of being unsafe as a physician?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s closer to it. Not in the sense that I think, “If I don’t review this three times, something will happen,” but more that if I don’t go back over it, I’m left with this feeling that I’m being irresponsible by not checking myself.\n\nEspecially with clinical things — even small ones. Like a wording choice in a note, or whether I framed something clearly enough to a patient, or whether I should have asked one more question even if, objectively, it was probably fine. I’ll replay it as if that little imperfection is evidence of something much bigger. Like it isn’t just “I could have done that slightly better,” it becomes, “Maybe this proves I don’t deserve the trust people are putting in me.”\n\nAnd that’s really the thing I’m here about, I think. I keep feeling like I’m not worthy of being trusted in that role. Like people see me as safe or competent because I have the title, but I know every small hesitation or imperfect choice underneath it. So when I review things, it’s partly anxiety, but it’s also guilt. It feels like if I stop looking at it, I’m letting myself off too easily.\n\nI don’t know if I’d say I feel “unsafe” exactly as a physician, but… I do sometimes feel like I could be dangerous because I’m not perfect. And then the reviewing becomes this attempt to prove that I’m not — or sometimes it just ends up proving the opposite in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or mental acts — like reviewing conversations, replaying events, or analyzing decisions — in an attempt to prevent feeling anxious or guilty, or to make sure you don’t make a mistake that could harm someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though it doesn’t feel as clean or intentional as “I’m doing this to prevent anxiety.” It feels more like I can’t let go of something because if I don’t examine it closely, then I’m somehow being careless.\n\nA lot of it is around work. I’ll replay a conversation with a patient, or a decision I made, or even something small in documentation, and it starts to feel much larger than it probably was. Like a small imperfection becomes evidence that I shouldn’t be trusted. I’ll think, “Why did I phrase it that way?” or “Should I have asked one more question?” or “What if I missed something because I was tired or distracted?” And then I’ll go through it again and again, trying to decide whether it was actually okay.\n\nBut it’s not reassuring for very long, if at all. Sometimes it just makes me feel worse, because the more I look at it, the more I find ways it could have been better. And then it turns into this feeling that maybe I’m dangerous in that role, not because there’s one clear mistake I can point to, but because I’m not perfect and people are trusting me anyway.\n\nSo… yes, I replay and analyze things. I think part of me is trying to make sure I didn’t harm someone or that I’m not going to. I don’t have a clear sense of, “I must do this review or something bad will happen,” like a rule in my head. It’s more vague than that.\n\nThere are times where if I don’t go back over something, I feel very unsettled, like I’m being negligent by moving on too quickly. And the guilt can feel… disproportionate, I guess. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed a direct link where I’m repeating the review specifically to prevent a bad outcome. It’s more that the question won’t leave me alone, and if I try to ignore it, it feels like I’m avoiding responsibility.\n\nSo yes, the reviews can feel hard to stop, and they do seem tied to fear of mistakes or guilt. But “in order to prevent something bad from happening” — I’m not completely sure. It doesn’t feel that organized in my mind. It feels more like I get stuck trying to find certainty, and I don’t really get it.\n\nDoctor: When you have these intrusive thoughts, do you feel a strong internal pressure to perform certain mental acts—like reviewing events, analyzing decisions, or replaying conversations—because you believe that not doing so could lead to increased anxiety, guilt, or a mistake that might harm someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… there is pressure, but I think it’s less like a clear command and more like this heavy internal demand that I can’t just move on. It’s not, “If I don’t replay this exact conversation, then X will happen,” but it’s more like, “If I don’t examine this, then I’m the kind of person who shouldn’t be trusted with patients.”\n\nThat’s really what I keep coming back to. I’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. And when I think about work, even very small clinical imperfections start to feel enormous. Something like whether I worded something the right way, or whether I paused too long, or whether I should have asked one more clarifying question — it doesn’t stay as just a small thing. I replay it as if it proves something about me, like it proves I’m dangerous as a doctor.\n\nSo yes, when the thought comes up, I do feel pushed to review it and analyze it. It’s more like I keep trying to reach a point where I can say, “Okay, that wasn’t dangerous, that doesn’t mean I failed,” but I don’t really get there.\n\nWhen something small happens clinically — or even when I just remember something later and wonder if it was imperfect — I can feel this need to go back over it in my mind. Not always because there’s a concrete error, but because it starts to feel like the imperfection itself means something about me. Like, if I phrased something badly, or didn’t think of every possible angle, then maybe that proves I shouldn’t be trusted.\n\nAnd that’s the part that feels intolerable, I guess. Am I being careless because I don’t want to face what I did?” So I keep turning it over, even when I’m tired of it, even when I know I’m probably not getting anywhere.\n\nI’m here because… I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. I can look at the chart afterward, and there may be nothing there — no clear bad outcome, no note that something went wrong, no evidence that anyone was harmed. And if someone else described the same situation to me, I might be able to say, “That was routine,” or “That delay was not dangerous,” or “That message wasn’t perfect, but it wasn’t harmful.”\n\nBut when it’s me, it doesn’t feel only like an anxious thought that’s separate from who I am. Like people see me as safe or competent because I have the title, but I know every small hesitation or imperfect choice underneath it. So when I review things, it’s partly anxiety, but it’s also guilt. It feels like if I stop looking at it, I’m letting myself off too easily.\n\nI don’t know if I’d say I feel “unsafe” exactly as a physician, but… I do sometimes feel like I could be dangerous because I’m not perfect. And then the reviewing becomes this attempt to prove that I’m not — or sometimes it just ends up proving the opposite in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or mental acts — like reviewing conversations, replaying events, or analyzing decisions — in an attempt to prevent feeling anxious or guilty, or to make sure you don’t make a mistake that could harm someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though it doesn’t feel as clean or intentional as “I’m doing this to prevent anxiety.” It feels more like I can’t let go of something because if I don’t examine it closely, then I’m somehow being careless.\n\nA lot of it is around work. I’ll replay a conversation with a patient, or a decision I made, or even something small in documentation, and it starts to feel much larger than it probably was. Like a small imperfection becomes evidence that I shouldn’t be trusted. I’ll think, “Why did I phrase it that way?” or “Should I have asked one more question?” or “What if I missed something because I was tired or distracted?” And then I’ll go through it again and again, trying to decide whether it was actually okay.\n\nBut it’s not reassuring for very long, if at all. Sometimes it just makes me feel worse, because the more I look at it, the more I find ways it could have been better. And then it turns into this feeling that maybe I’m dangerous in that role, not because there’s one clear mistake I can point to, but because I’m not perfect and people are trusting me anyway.\n\nSo… yes, I replay and analyze things. I think part of me is trying to make sure I didn’t harm someone or that I’m not going to. But underneath that, it’s more this guilt, this feeling that I don’t deserve the trust people put in me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that you have to repeat these mental reviews or analyses in order to prevent something bad from happening, like making a mistake that could harm someone or feeling unbearable guilt?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it that strongly. I don’t have a clear sense of, “I must do this review or something bad will happen,” like a rule in my head. It’s more vague than that.\n\nThere are times where if I don’t go back over something, I feel very unsettled, like I’m being negligent by moving on too quickly. And the guilt can feel… disproportionate, I guess. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed a direct link where I’m repeating the review specifically to prevent a bad outcome. It’s more that the question won’t leave me alone, and if I try to ignore it, it feels like I’m avoiding responsibility.\n\nSo yes, the reviews can feel hard to stop, and they do seem tied to fear of mistakes or guilt. But “in order to prevent something bad from happening” — I’m not completely sure. It doesn’t feel that organized in my mind. It feels more like I get stuck trying to find certainty, and I don’t really get it.\n\nDoctor: When you have these intrusive thoughts, do you feel a strong internal pressure to perform certain mental acts—like reviewing events, analyzing decisions, or replaying conversations—because you believe that not doing so could lead to increased anxiety, guilt, or a mistake that might harm someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… there is pressure, but I think it’s less like a clear command and more like this heavy internal demand that I can’t just move on. It’s not, “If I don’t replay this exact conversation, then X will happen,” but it’s more like, “If I don’t examine this, then I’m the kind of person who shouldn’t be trusted with patients.”\n\nThat’s really what I keep coming back to. I’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. And when I think about work, even very small clinical imperfections start to feel enormous. Something like whether I worded something the right way, or whether I paused too long, or whether I should have asked one more clarifying question — it doesn’t stay as just a small thing. I replay it as if it proves something about me, like it proves I’m dangerous as a doctor.\n\nSo yes, when the thought comes up, I do feel pushed to review it and analyze it. I think part of me is trying to reduce the guilt, or make sure I didn’t miss something that could hurt someone. But honestly, a lot of the time it feels like I’m trying to answer this bigger question of whether I should be in this role at all. And the reviewing doesn’t really settle it. It just keeps circling around the same fear: that people trust me because of my title, but if they saw all the small doubts and imperfections underneath, they wouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you absolutely must finish reviewing a thought or situation in your mind, even if you don’t want to, because not doing so would feel intolerable or would mean you’re failing in your responsibility as a doctor?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s close, though the word “finish” is difficult, because I don’t know that it ever feels finished. It’s more like I keep trying to reach a point where I can say, “Okay, that wasn’t dangerous, that doesn’t mean I failed,” but I don’t really get there.\n\nWhen something small happens clinically — or even when I just remember something later and wonder if it was imperfect — I can feel this need to go back over it in my mind. Not always because there’s a concrete error, but because it starts to feel like the imperfection itself means something about me. Like, if I phrased something badly, or didn’t think of every possible angle, then maybe that proves I shouldn’t be trusted.\n\nAnd that’s the part that feels intolerable, I guess. Not just anxiety, but the guilt of moving on as if it didn’t matter. I’ll think, “If I stop reviewing this, am I just excusing myself? Am I being careless because I don’t want to face what I did?” So I keep turning it over, even when I’m tired of it, even when I know I’m probably not getting anywhere.\n\nI’m here because… I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. And when I replay these small clinical imperfections, they don’t stay small. They start to feel like evidence that I’m a dangerous doctor, or that I’m pretending to be safe and competent when maybe I’m not. So yes, sometimes stopping the review feels like failing in my responsibility, even if I can also see that the reviewing itself isn’t really helping.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that you have to keep analyzing or replaying certain thoughts or events in your mind, even when you don’t want to, because not doing so would increase your anxiety or make you feel like you're at fault or responsible for something bad happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, it’s not always as neat as “if I don’t replay this, then something bad will happen,” but emotionally it feels close to that. If I don’t go back over it, I feel like I’m letting myself off too easily, or like I’m ignoring something I should be taking responsibility for.\n\nA lot of it is tied to work. I’m here because I feel like I don’t really deserve the trust people put in me. And then these small things—small clinical imperfections, things that maybe another person would just learn from and move on from—become huge in my head. I’ll replay whether I said something the right way, whether I missed a nuance, whether I should have asked one more question, whether my judgment was as careful as it should have been. And it starts to feel like those little things are not just little things, but evidence that I’m dangerous as a doctor.\n\nSo when I keep analyzing it, part of me is trying to figure out if I’m at fault, or if I’ve failed someone, or if I’m the kind of person who shouldn’t be trusted in that role. And if I stop before I’ve somehow resolved it—which I usually don’t—it feels irresponsible. Like I’m choosing comfort over accountability.\n\nI don’t want to keep doing it. It’s exhausting and it doesn’t really reassure me for long. But stopping can feel worse, because then I’m left with this sense that maybe I’m just avoiding the truth about myself.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up about not being good enough or being dangerous as a doctor, do they feel out of line with how you see yourself, or do they feel like something you actually believe is true about you?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to answer that, because part of me knows how extreme it sounds when I say it out loud. I can look at the chart afterward, and there may be nothing there — no clear bad outcome, no note that something went wrong, no evidence that anyone was harmed. And if someone else described the same situation to me, I might be able to say, “That was routine,” or “That delay was not dangerous,” or “That message wasn’t perfect, but it wasn’t harmful.”\n\nBut when it’s me, it doesn’t feel only like an anxious thought that’s separate from who I am. It starts to feel true. Like there’s this quiet conviction that I’ve harmed someone in some subtle way and it just hasn’t been discovered yet.\n\nI’ll think about some routine delay, or a message I sent that wasn’t worded perfectly, or something I didn’t respond to as quickly or as completely as I think I should have. And even if I review it and there’s no sign of harm, I still keep thinking, “No, something happened because of this. Someone was affected. It just hasn’t surfaced yet.” And then I imagine that eventually everyone is going to find out — the patient, the team, whoever — and they’ll see that I was careless or dangerous all along.\n\nSo I guess… it feels both out of line and not out of line. Intellectually, I can see that the evidence isn’t there. But emotionally, and sometimes more than emotionally, it feels like something I actually believe about myself: that I’m not just imperfect, but that I’ve quietly hurt someone and it’s only a matter of time before it’s discovered.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts about being a dangerous or unworthy doctor come up, do they feel like something foreign to you that you don’t truly believe, or do they feel like they fit with how you see yourself as a person or professional?\n\nPatient: It feels like it fits, unfortunately. I don’t like saying that, because I know there’s a version of me that can step back and say, “You’re taking small things and making them mean something enormous.” But when I’m actually in it, it doesn’t feel foreign, like some random thought that’s obviously separate from me. It feels like it’s revealing something true.\n\nI’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. That’s really the center of it. People come to me assuming I’m careful, competent, safe — and when I replay these small clinical imperfections, they start to feel like proof that I’m not. Like if I missed a nuance, or worded something poorly, or didn’t think through every possible implication, then maybe that isn’t just being human. Maybe it means I’m dangerous, or unworthy of the role.\n\nAnd I know that may sound disproportionate. I can see that. But it doesn’t land as “just anxiety” in the moment. It feels consistent with this deeper fear I have about myself professionally — that I’m not deserving of the confidence patients and colleagues place in me. So no, it doesn’t feel foreign exactly. It feels painfully believable.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you have to mentally reanalyze or re-examine past decisions in detail — even if not in a fixed way — as a way to manage the anxiety that comes up when these intrusive thoughts occur?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. It’s not always a formal checklist or anything — I don’t sit there and say, “Now I will review step one, step two, step three.” But I end up doing that in practice. I’ll go back through a decision or an interaction in this very detailed way, almost like I’m auditing myself, trying to find the point where I failed.\n\nAnd it’s usually these small things. A phrase I used with a patient, or whether I responded quickly enough, or whether I should have considered something one step further. On paper, it might look minor. But in my head it becomes this whole chain of implications: if I missed that nuance, then maybe I wasn’t careful; if I wasn’t careful, maybe I harmed someone; if I harmed someone, then maybe I shouldn’t be trusted as a doctor.\n\nSo yes, the reanalyzing is partly about anxiety. I think I’m trying to reduce it by proving to myself that I didn’t do something wrong. But it doesn’t really work, because the more I replay it, the more it starts to feel like evidence against me. Like these small imperfections are actually proof that I’m dangerous or that I don’t deserve the trust people put in me.\n\nAnd if I try to stop before I’ve gone through it enough, I feel this pressure, like I’m being irresponsible or letting myself avoid accountability. So I keep turning it over and over, even though I know it’s exhausting and usually doesn’t lead anywhere reassuring.\n\nDoctor: When you have these intrusive thoughts about being a dangerous or unworthy doctor, do you feel driven to perform certain mental acts — like reviewing events, analyzing decisions, or replaying conversations — because you believe that not doing them could lead to a mistake that harms someone or makes you feel even more guilty?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that is a big part of it. It’s not just that I review things because I’m curious or because I want to learn from them in a normal professional way. It feels driven, like I’m supposed to go back through every small imperfection until I can be sure I didn’t miss something harmful.\n\nAnd the reason I’m here is really that I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. So when some small clinical thing happens — a message I sent, a delay, a phrase I used, a decision that maybe could have been more complete — I don’t experience it as just a minor imperfection. I start replaying it like it might be proof that I’m a dangerous doctor.\n\nIf I don’t review it, I feel like I’m being careless all over again. Like not rechecking it means I’m choosing to ignore the possibility that I harmed someone, or that I could harm someone. And then the guilt gets worse, because it feels like I’m letting myself off the hook when I don’t deserve to be let off the hook.\n\nSo yes, I do feel pushed to analyze and replay things. I’ll go over the sequence in my head — what I said, what I didn’t say, what I should have noticed, what someone might have taken from it — and I’m trying to get to a place where I can say, “No, this was safe, this was okay.” But often it doesn’t reassure me. It just makes the whole thing feel more serious, like the fact that I have to examine it so much means there must be something there."
